ВЗАМЕННЫЕ БЛАГОВОДИЯ  Брюсу Стерлингу, Льюису Шайнеру, Джону Ширли, героям. И Тому Мэддоксу, изобретателю EIS. И другим, кто знает почему.

Кейс закрыл глаза. Нашел переключатель с рифленой поверхностью. И в темноте, пропитанной кровью, за глазами вспыхнули серебряные фосфоресцентные образы на границах пространства, гипнотические изображения, напоминающие фрагментально смонтированный фильм, который шел вперед и назад. Символы, цифры, лица, размытое, фрагментарное мандала визуальной информации. "Пожалуйста", - он молил, - "теперь...". Серебристая пластина, цвет неба Чибы. "Теперь..." Диск начал вращаться, все быстрее и быстрее, превращаясь в светло-серую сферу. Расширялся. И раскрывался для него. Как трюк оригами, который раскрывается в жидком неоне, его безграничная родина, его страна, прозрачная шахматная доска в 3D, бесконечно простирающаяся. Внутреннее глазо открылось к отполированной, ярко-красной пирамиде Восточной прибрежной атомной электростанции, возвышающейся над зелеными кубами банка Америки Митсубиси. Высокое и очень отдаленное место. Он видел спирали военных систем, навсегда недоступные для него. И где-то там, смеясь, в белоснежном потолочном помещении, с нежными пальцами на палубе, лицо залитое слезами радости. С любовью Деб, которая сделала это возможным.

ПЕРВЫЙ ЧАСТЬ  Чибская городская меланхолия

Небо над гаванью имело цвет дальнего предвидения, настроенного на мертвый канал. "Это уже не то", - услышал Кейс, пробираясь плечами через толпу у двери чатба. "Просто так, как будто ты развил огромную зависимость от наркотиков". Это был голос спроула и шутка спроула*. Чатсубо был баром для постоянных иностранцев в добровольном изгнании; там можно было выпить неделю, не услышав ни слова по-японски. Рэтц хлопнул кулаком по стойке. Его протез руки монотонно дрожал, когда он наполнял стаканы Кирином из бочки. Он улыбнулся Кейсу. Его зубы были странным сооружением из восточноевропейского стали и шокирующего коричневого гниения. Кейс нашел место за стойкой между непредсказуемой красотой проститутки Лонни Зоны и жесткой униформой морского офицера, чья щека была прорезана точными рядами племенных шрамов. "Он уже был здесь с двумя попутчиками", - сказал Рэтц и протолкнул Кейсу стакан пива через стойку. "Делай что-нибудь со мной, Кейс?"

Кейс пожимал плечами. Девушка справа от него хихикнула. Бармен улыбнулся. Его уродство было легкомысленным. В эпоху покупательной красоты его недостаток имел сигнальное значение. Древний протез рук стонал, когда он хватался за другой стакан. Это был русский военный протез, захватчик с семью функциями, * "спроул" - это слово для обширного, нерегулярно раскинувшегося района с наркотиками. - Перевод 8

управляемый обратной связью, электрический и встраиваемый в грязное розовое пластик. "Ты художник, Кейс", - рычал Рэтц, звук которого начинался как его смех. Он потирал себе розовую лапу по белому рубашке, подтягивая выпуклый живот. "Ты жонглируешь странными сделками".

"Конечно", - сказал Кейс и глотнул пива. "Кто-то здесь должен быть странным. Ты точно не тот, черт возьми. Так что убирайся, хорошо?" Зона - мой личный друг, - сказала она.

Она посмотрела Кейсу в глаза и извергла самый легкий спутник слюны, не двигая губами. Но она ушла.

"Боже мой!" - сказал Кейс. "Какое это ужасное дурное место? Даже пива выпить нельзя".

"Ха", - сказал Рэтц, протирая деревянную доску мокрой тряпкой, - Зона приходит с процентами. Ты можешь сюда заходить ради развлечения.

Когда Кейс потянулся за своим стаканом, наступил один из тех странных моментов молчания, когда, как будто сто отдельных разговоров одновременно пришли в паузу. Затем раздался хихиканье проститутки, наполненный какой-то гистерией.

Рэтц рычал. "Это призрак".

"Китайцы", - крикнул пьяный австралийец, - "Китайцы изобрели эту проклятую нервную дрянь. Если это что-то с нервами, я еду на материк...".

"Ну и что", - сказал Кейс своему стакану, и его вся обида поднялась в нем, как желчь, - "это просто чепуха".

Японцы уже сделали больше нейрохирургических операций, чем когда-либо могли сделать китайцы. Черные клиники Чибы были на высоте; каждый месяц заменялось целое процедуры. Тем не менее, они не смогли исправить вред, нанесенный Кейсу в отеле Мемфиса.

После года здесь он по-прежнему мечтал о киберпространстве, и его надежда угасала с каждой ночью. Все стимуляторы, которые он принимал, все прогулки по узким улочкам и уголкам Ночной Города помогали ему ничего. Он все еще видел в сне матрицу, яркие решетки логики, раскрывающиеся над пустым цветным пустым пространством... Спроул теперь лежал странно далеко за горизонтом его сознания, и он не был консоль-гьером, ни киберпространственным ковбоем. Он был всего лишь маленьким вором среди многих, пытающимся пробиться сквозь это. Но в японской ночи сны обрушивались на него как водный заклинание на проводной проволоке, и тогда он плакал, плакал во сне, когда лежал один в темноте своей капсулы в гостинице Сарг, прижимаясь к кровати и борясь с пузырьками температуры, чтобы добраться до консоли, которая не существовала.

"Я видел твою девушку прошлой ночью", - сказал Рэтц, толкнув Кейса вторым подбородком.

"У меня нет девушки", - ответил он и выпил.

"Мисс Линда Ли".

Кейс покачал головой.

"Никакой девушки? Ничего? Только бизнес, друг художник? Полностью преданный коммерции?" "You made me like you better, laughed more. I fear one night you'll become too artificial; end up in a clinic cell, spare parts warehouse." "You're breaking my heart, Ratz." He drained his beer, paid and left, his tall, slim shoulders spread wide under the rain-sodden khaki nylon anorak. As he squeezed through the crowd of ninseis, he smelled his own pungent sweat. Case was twenty-four. At twenty-two, he'd been a cowboy, an active one, one of the best in the sprawl. He'd been trained by the best: McCoy Pauley and Bobby Quine, legends in the game. With constant adrenaline overload, side effects of his youth and skill, he clung to a standard Kyberspace deck that projected his de-materialized consciousness into the reflective hallucination of the Matrix. As a thief, he worked for others, richer thieves, employers who supplied the exotic software needed to pierce gleaming corporate facades and open windows into rich data fields. He made the classic mistake, though he'd sworn never to; he stole from his employers. He kept something for himself and tried to sell it through a fence in Amsterdam. He wasn't entirely sure how they'd caught him, but that didn't matter anymore. He was expecting death, but they just smiled. "Of course you're welcome," they said, "welcome in the realm of money." And he needed the money. Indeed, still smiling, they would make sure he could never work again. They damaged his nervous system with a Russian mushroom toxin from wartime.

In a Memphis hotel room tied to a bed, he lay half-conscious for thirty hours. Talent burned away micron by micron. The damage was slight, unnoticeable but extremely effective. For Case, who'd lived for the bodiless pleasures of cyberspace, it was a major fall. In the bars where he used to socialize as a super-cowboy, the elite of the industry showed a certain detachment and disrespect towards flesh. The body was just flesh. Case became a prisoner of his own flesh. All his possessions were quickly exchanged for new yen, crisp notes of the old paper currency that circulated endlessly through the closed channels of the global black market like the marine shells of the Trobriand Islanders. It was difficult to conduct legal transactions in cash within the sprawl; it was already banned in Japan. In Japan, he had stubbornly convinced himself, he would find a hundred percent cure. In Chiba. Either in a registered clinic or in the gray zone of black medicine. Chiba, a synonym for plantations, nerve splices and microbionics, was a magnet for techno-criminal subcultures of the sprawl.

In Chiba, he'd watched his new yen disappear over two months of tests and consultations. The people in the black clinics, his last hope, had stared at his disfigured body and then slowly shook their heads. Now he slept in the cheapest boxes, those right by the docks, between the quartz halo-beam lights that illuminated the docks day and night like giant stages; where because of the bright distant light you couldn't see Tokyo's holographic logo of Fuji Electric Company, nor even the sky-high hologram, and Tokyo Bay presented itself as a black desert, with gulls circling over the floating white styrofoam scraps.

Behind the harbor lay the city with its factory domes, towering over the vast cubes of company-owned arcologies*. Harbor and city were separated by a narrow strip of old streets, a no man's land without an official name. Night City with Ninsei at its core. By day, the Ninsei bars were closed behind iron roller shutters and unassuming; the neon lights were off, the holograms dead, in standby under the toxic silver sky.

Two blocks west of Chat in a teahouse, Case washed down the first pill of the evening with a double espresso. It was a flat pink octagon, a strong-acting Brazilian Dexedrin he'd bought from one of Zone's girls. The teahouse had mirrored walls framed in red neon. In Chiba, with little money and even less hope for a cure, he'd initially fallen into some kind of overdrive reaction. With a cold determination that didn't suit his nature at all, he set out to get money. In the first month, he killed two men and a woman - for sums he would have considered absurd a year ago. Ninsei was consuming him until the street itself seemed to embody a death wish, working like a hidden poison he carried around carelessly.

Night City resembled a deranged experiment in adapted Darwinism, designed by a bored researcher who kept his finger on the FF* button. Stop acquiring money and you disappear without a trace; move too fast and you break through the fragile surface tension of the black market and get served as well; in both cases, all that remains of you is a vague memory in someone like Ratz's mind, although your heart or lungs or kidneys can certainly keep on living for another stranger's needs against new yen for an organ bank.

Everything smelled of business here. Death was the known penalty for inaction, carelessness, unsavory tactics, failure to meet the demands of a twisted protocol. As Case sat alone at a table in the teahouse and slammed the octagon down, sweat beads broke out on his palms and every tickling hair on his arms and chest suddenly became noticeable, he realized he was playing a game again, an ancient one without a name, a final solitaire. He wasn't carrying a weapon anymore, didn't care about basic safety precautions.

He made the fastest, loosest deals on the street and was known for being able to get anything desired. A part of him knew that his bright light was obvious evidence of self-destruction to his dwindling clients, but the same... * FF = Fast Forward = Schnellvorlauf. - The translation

On a Friday night in Ninsai, he passed by yakitori stands and massage salons, a franchise café named Beautiful Girl, the electronic roar of an arcade. He moved to the side to let a slender man in a sarariman suit pass, as he noticed the Mitsubishi Genentech logo tattooed on his right palm. Was it authentic? If so, Case thought, trouble was coming. If not, he deserved what he got. MG employees at a certain level had modern microprocessors implanted that regulated their mutagen levels in their bloodstreams. Such devices in Night City brought trouble, got you snatched up by a black clinic. The sarariman was Japanese, but the people in Ninsai were Gaijin. Groups of sailors from the port across the bay, individual tourists desperately seeking thrills not listed in any travel guide, heavy guys from the sprawl showing off their transplants and implants, and a dozen different species of crooks all swarmed the street in a complex dance of lust and commerce. There were countless theories about why Chiba City tolerated the enclave of Ninsai, but Case leaned towards the idea that the Yakuza kept it as some sort of historical park, a monument to its shady beginnings. On the other hand, he also understood that increasing technological specialization needed free spaces, that Night City didn't exist for its inhabitants... * Yakitori = Japanese dish, grilled chicken in soy sauce. - The translation ** Mutagens = chemical substances that cause mutations. - The translation *** Gaijin = Japanese: foreigner. - The translation **** Yakuza = Japanese gangsters. - The translation 19

He wondered if Linda was right when he looked up at the lights. Would Wage kill him to set an example? It didn't make logical sense, but of course Wage mainly dealt in prescription bio-ware, and to do something like that, they said, you had to be crazy. But Linda said Wage wanted to have him killed. Case's first insight into street deals was that he wasn't really needed by either the buyer or seller. The middleman's job is to become a necessary evil. The shady niche Case created in Night City's criminal ecosystem came through lies and deceptions, night after night. Now that he felt its walls beginning to crumble, he experienced a strange euphoria. Last week, he had delayed the transfer of a synthetic gland extract and sold it at a higher margin than usual. He knew Wage hadn't liked that. Wage, who had been in Chiba for nine years and was one of the few Gaijin dealers who managed to establish contacts with the rigidly hierarchical criminal establishment beyond Night City's borders, was his most important supplier. Gen and hormone material flowed through a tangled network of straw men into Ninsai. For some reason, Wage once managed to trace something back, and now he enjoyed steady relationships in a dozen cities. Case caught himself staring at a shop window. The store sold trinkets to sailors: watches, folding knives, lighters, pocket video recorders, stim decks, weighted manriki chains, and shuriken. The shuriken, steel stars with razor-sharp points, fascinated him. Some were chrome-plated, others black, some had a rainbow-like film like oil on water coating them. But the chrome-plated stars held his gaze. They were fastened with almost invisible nylon threads on crimson ultravelour and embossed in the center with dragons or Yin Yang symbols. They reflected the neon light from the street distorted. Case felt these were his guiding stars, his fate, depicted by a constellation of cheap chrome. "Julie," he said to them. "Time to visit old Julie. He'll know." Julius Deane, whose metabolism was vigorously adjusted each week with a fortune in serums and hormones, was 153 years old. His main protection against aging consisted of an annual pilgrimage to Tokyo, where gene technicians re-programmed his DNA code, a procedure unavailable in Chiba. After that, he always flew to Hong Kong to get custom-made suits and shirts for the whole year. Impotent and uncomplaining, it seemed he found satisfaction mostly through his obsession with extravagant clothing. Case had never seen him wear the same suit twice, although he exclusively wore precise replicas of 20th century fashion. He liked to present himself with a thin gold-framed glasses with pink synthetic quartz lenses, faceted like mirrors in a Victorian dollhouse.

His office was located in a warehouse behind Ninsai. Some rooms had been sparsely furnished years ago with a colorful collection of European furniture, as if Deane once intended to use them as an apartment. Neo-Aztec bookcases, collecting dust, lined the walls of the room where Case waited. Two bulky, belly-shaped lamps in Disney style stood on a low side table covered in red-lacquered steel, in the Kandinsky look. A Dali clock hung between the bookcases on the wall. The distorted dial extended to the bare concrete floor. The hands were holograms that changed in sync with the distorted dial as it turned, but never showed the correct time. The room was filled with stackable white fiberglas shipping boxes that smelled of candied ginger. "Looks clean, old man," Deane's gravelly voice said. "Come on in!" The massive door with rosewood imitation to the left of the bookcases slid back on magnetic catches. On the plastic veneer, JULIUS DEANE IMPORT EXPORT was written in large self-adhesive letters that were peeling off. If the furniture in Deane's makeshift foyer dated back to the end of the last century, the actual office seemed to be from the beginning. Deane's smooth, pink face stared at him from the light beam of an antique brass lamp with a dark green glass shade. The importer was hidden behind a massive desk made of lacquered steel, squeezed in on both sides by high wooden cabinets with many drawers. File cabinets, Case suspected, as they were in pre-electronic times for storing written documents...

With his hands in his jacket pockets, Case stared through the glass at a flat rectangle of lab-grown flesh on a beautifully crafted pedestal made of false jade. The color of the skin reminded him of one of Zones' prostitutes; she had been tattooed with a glowing digital display powered by a subcutaneous chip. He wondered involuntarily, as sweat broke out on his ribs, why he needed to carry something like that in his pocket when he could just as well keep it in his bag?

Without moving his head, Case scanned the crowd through the reflective window. There.

Behind sailors in short-sleeved khaki. Dark hair, mirrored glasses, dark clothes, slender... And gone. Now Case ducked and weaved through the crowd at a diagonal.

'Can you lend me a gun, Shin?'

The boy smiled. 'Two hours.'

They stood together in the smell of fresh, raw seafood behind a sushi stall in Shiga. 'Back in two hours.'

Shin rummaged through empty two-liter cans that had once held sea salt powder. He pulled out a thin bundle wrapped in gray plastic wrap. 'Taser.** 30 new yen deposit.’

The boy shrugged and tucked the bundle behind the sea salt cans. 'Two hours.'

He went into the store without even looking at the displayed shurikens. He had never thrown one before.

He bought two packs of Yeheyuan with a Mitsubi Bank chip that identified him as Charles Derek May. The best chip he'd ever gotten, beating Truman Starr.

The Japanese woman behind the terminal looked like she hadn't slept in years, though without any scientific backing. He pulled out a thin roll from under the counter and showed it to her. 'I want to buy a weapon.'

She pointed to a display case filled with knives.

'No,' he said. 'I don’t like knives.'

She pulled out a long, rectangular box from under the counter. The yellow cardboard lid was printed with a stylized rolled-up cobra with its neck spread wide open. Inside were eight identical, wrapped cylinders. He watched as her speckled, brown fingers peeled one of them from the paper. She held it up for inspection, a matte steel tube with a leather strap on one end and a small bronze pyramid at the other. She gripped the tube in one hand, took the pyramid between her thumb and forefinger of the other hand and pulled. Three oiled, extendable spirals shot out telescopically and snapped shut.

'Cobra,' she said.

25

Over the neon lights of Ninsei, the sky was a simple gray. The air was worse; that evening it seemed to have teeth. Half the crowd wore respirator masks. Case had tried out his Cobra for ten minutes in a public restroom, trying to figure out how to discreetly carry it with him; he'd ended up just stuffing the grip into the waistband of his jeans, so the tube lay across his stomach. The pyramid-shaped tip dangled between his chest and the fabric of his anorak. He constantly felt like it would clatter to the ground on his next step, but it gave him a better feeling.

The bar wasn't really a place for dealing, but it attracted relevant customers on weekdays. Fridays and Saturdays evenings were different. Regulars were mostly there too, but they disappeared in the crowd of sailors and specialists who lurked around them. When Case pushed open the swing door, he looked for Ratz but the bartender was nowhere to be seen. Lonny Zone, the bar's regular pimp, followed a young sailor with glassy, fatherly eyes as one of his girls began to work him over. Zone relied on a hypnotic drug that Japanese called 'cloud dancers'. When Case caught Zone’s eye, he signaled him to come over. In slow motion, Zone moved through the crowd, his long face slack and relaxed.

'Have you seen Wage tonight, Lonny?'

Zone looked at him as always, unperturbed, and shook his head. 'Sure, man?'

'Maybe in Namban. Two hours ago maybe.'

'A couple of guys with him? One of them thin, dark hair, maybe black jacket?'

'No,' said Zone finally, furrowing his brow to indicate the effort it cost him to remember such trivia. 'Big guys. Transplants.'

Zone's eyes had very little white and even less iris; his pupils were widely dilated under flabby eyelids. He looked Case up and down for a long time before looking away. He noticed the bulge of the steel whip. 'Cobra,' he said, raising an eyebrow. 'Whipping someone’s ass?'

'See you later, Lonny!' Case left the bar.

His shadow was back. Case was certain. A mix of octagon and adrenaline gave him a thrill. You like this, he told himself; you're crazy.

Because strangely, it was almost like running in the Matrix. If you get sidetracked and realize you're hopelessly stuck, you might see Ninsei as a data field, reminding you of proteins once linked by the Matrix to distinguish cell features. Then you could move at high speed, skimming over it, completely detached yet unattached from everything, surrounded by the buzz of business, interacting information, flesh-and-blood data in the labyrinth of the black market... Just do it, Case! he told himself. Put them in. They'd least expect that. He was a block away from the game hell where he'd first met Linda Lee.

He stormed through Ninsei, scattering a horde of meandering sailors. One of them cursed him in Spanish. Then he was through the entrance. The noise crashed over him like a wave, bass pulsing in his gut. Every landing would be a 10-megaton hit in Europe's tank war; a simulated explosion filled the arcade with a bright roar as a dark red fireball hologram bloomed mushroom-like. He swerved to the right and ran up a staircase of unvarnished plywood. He'd been here once before, to discuss a deal with prescription hormone triggers with a certain Matsuga. He remembered the corridor, the grimy linoleum, the row of identical doors leading to tiny offices. One door was now open. A young Japanese woman in a sleeveless black T-shirt looked up from a white terminal; behind her head hung a poster of Greece's Aegean Sea, with snappy lettering. "Watch out!" Case warned her.

Then he ran through the corridor until he was out of sight. The last two doors were closed and, as he suspected, locked. He spun around and slammed his nylon sneakers against the blue-painted plywood door at the rear end. It banged open. Cheap metal clattered from the splintered frame. Inside, dark, the white round of a monitor housing. He was already at the door to its right, wrapping both hands around the transparent plastic button and pushing with all his might. It cracked, and he was inside. Here, Case and Wage had met Matsuga, but the shell company Matsuga ran wasn't there anymore. No terminal, nothing. Light from an alley behind the arcade filtered through smoky plastic. He could make out a twisted strand of fiber optic cables poking out of a box in the wall, a pile of discarded food containers, and the wingless basket of an electric fan.

The window consisted of a single sheet of cheap plastic. He shed his anorak, wrapped it around his right hand, and hit. The pane shattered and fell after two more hits. The muffled din of the arcade was drowned out by a siren, either from the broken glass or the girl at the end of the corridor.

Case turned, put on his anorak again, and pulled the cobra completely out. Since the door was closed, he figured his pursuer assumed he'd vanished through the one he'd half-ripped off its hinges. The bronze pyramid of the cobra began to sway slightly as the steel spring bit into his pulse.

Nothing happened. There was only the siren wail, the arcade noise, his heartbeat. Fear hit like a long-forgotten friend. Not the cold, agile mechanism of Dex paranoia, but raw, animal fear. He'd lived in constant dread for so long he'd forgotten what naked fear felt like.

This little room was a place where people kept dying. Maybe he should die here. Maybe they had guns.

A muffled rumble from the other end of the corridor. A male voice in Japanese. A scream, shrill and piercing. More muffled noise.

And footsteps approaching without haste.

They reached his door. Three quick heartbeats, then they stopped and reversed. One, two, three. The heel of a boot scraped on the linoleum. His adrenaline-fueled courage crumbled completely. He whipped the cobra into its sheath and darted to the window, blind with fear, utterly drained. He was outside, falling before he knew what he was doing. A dull pain shot through his shins when he hit the pavement.

A thin beam of light from a half-open service shaft framed a pile of fiber optic cables and the casing of a broken console. He landed face down on a damp piece of plywood; he rolled onto his back, in the shadow of the console. The window of the office cubicle was a dimly lit rectangle. The siren still wailed, louder here as the rear wall muffled the arcade noise.

A head appeared in the window, illuminated from behind by fluorescent lights in the corridor. Although the head reappeared, he couldn't make out its features. Silver gleam over the eyes. "Fuck," someone said, a woman with a northern sprawl accent. The head disappeared. Case lay under the console and counted to twenty. Then he stood up. He still held the steel cobra, but it took him a few seconds to remember what it was. He hobbled away down the alley, rubbing his left ankle.

Shins' pistol was a 50-year-old Vietnamese imitation of a South American copy of a Walther PPK, double action system with a hard-to-pull trigger and a .22 rifle chamber. Case would have preferred lead azide explosive rounds rather than the simple Chinese cartridges Shin had sold him. At least it was a gun with nine rounds of ammunition. As he passed by the sushi stand in Shiga, he weighed it in his anorak pocket. The grip was made of bright red plastic shaped like an upright dragon, a motif that could be felt in the dark. He'd left the cobra in a dumpster in Ninsei and swallowed another octagon dry.

The pill lit up his circuits, and he hurried from Shiga to Ninsei, then on to Baiitsu. His pursuer was gone, which was good. He had calls to make, business to take care of, things that couldn't wait. Behind the first block of Baiitsu, heading towards the harbor, stood an unassuming ten-story office building with a hideous yellow brick facade. Its windows were now dark, but a faint light could be seen on the roof if you strained your neck. A turned-off neon sign at the main entrance proclaimed CHEAP HOTEL under a flickering "A."

He knew that the implanted electrodes and the small plastic stun-band that hung from a stim deck were basically the same thing, and the kyberspace matrix was a significant simplification of human sensory input at least in terms of representation; but stim seemed to him an imposed multiplication of physical inputs. The commercial stuff was obviously edited, so you wouldn't feel anything if Tally Isham got a headache in the middle of it.

They found their paradise, a "pirate's paradise" tucked away at the convoluted edge of a relatively unsecured academic grid. At first glance it resembled the graffiti that computer students sometimes leave at the intersections of grid lines: blurred glyphs of colored light glistening before the chaotic twists of a dozen art faculties. "There," said the flatline, "the blue one. You see? That's an entry code for Bell Europa. Still fresh. Bell will be in soon and read the whole damn plan and change found codes. The new ones will be stolen by the boys tomorrow." Case tapped his way into Bell Europa and switched to a normal phone line. He had a hangover and was confused. With his eyes closed, he tried piecing together the various parts of a story about a certain Corto. Hosaka had sifted through the sparse data and put together an overview that, however, contained numerous gaps. The material included printouts from some publications that Hosaka quickly - too quickly - scrolled across the screen, so Case had to ask the computer to read the text to him. Also among them were audio recordings of the Fist-Screaming negotiation. Willis Corto, a colonel, had penetrated through a blind spot in Russia's defense at Kirensk. The transporters created a hole with impulse bombs and Corto's team was dropped off by Nightwing microlights. The rigid wings buzzed in the moonlight reflecting off the Angara and Podhamennaya rivers - the last light Corto would see for fifteen months. Case tried to imagine the microlights unfolding from their carrier capsules high above the frozen steppe. "You gave us quite a runaround, boss," said Case as Molly rolled around next to him. The microlights were unarmed, discarded to make room for the weight of a console operator, a special deck and a virus program called Mole IX that was the first real virus in the history of cybernetics. Corto and his team had trained for three years for this mission... 109

...when the EMPs went off. The Russian impulse cannons plunged the jockeys into electronic darkness; the Nightwings were finished, their onboard electronics failed. Then lasers opened fire, homing in on infrared the fragile, radar-transparent attack aircraft from the air, and Corto and his dead console operator fell from the Siberian sky. They kept falling... The story had gaps when Case looked at documents regarding a hijacked Russian gunship that could set down in Finland. To be torn apart by an antiquated 20-millimeter cannon manned by a reserve cadre on morning readiness duty, in a pine forest. Screaming Fist ended for Corto on the outskirts of Helsinki, where Finnish medics cut him out of the helicopter's wreckage. The war ended nine days later, and Corto, blind, legless and with almost completely missing jaw - was transferred to a military hospital in Utah. It took the congressional representative nearly eleven months to find him there. He listened to the dripping of drained tubes. In Washington and McLean, the trials had already begun. The Pentagon and CIA were balkanized, partially dismantled, and a congressional committee focused its attention on Screaming Fist. "Ready for Watergate," noted the representative to Corto. "He needs eyes, legs and extensive cosmetic work," said the representative, but that could be arranged. "Plumber's case," added the man and pressed Corto's shoulder through the damp sheet. 110

Corto listened to the quiet, unceasing dripping. He said he wanted to testify as he was. "No," explained the representative, "the negotiations will be broadcast on TV. The trial must appeal to the voter." The representative coughed politely. Restored, re-equipped and thoroughly prepared, Corto then gave testimony. His statements were detailed, compelling, clear - mostly an invention of a congressional clique who had a vested interest in preserving certain parts of Pentagon infrastructure. Corto gradually realized that his statements were used to save the careers of three officials directly responsible for suppressing reports on the construction of the EMP defense at Kirensk. After playing his part in the trials, he was no longer welcome in Washington. In a restaurant on M Street, the representative explained over crepes au sarrasin the great risk one took when talking to the wrong people. Corto rammed his stretched fingers into the throat of the man. Choking, the congressional representative face-planted onto the crepe au sarrasin while Corto walked out of Washington in the cool September air. — Этот маленький хулиган почти справился, — сказал финн, чье колено щелкнуло при подъеме. Он поправил свои брюки. — Посмотри на этот беспорядок, — продолжил он, — это не был обычный ресторан. Настоящая красота. Так что помогай им убрать это и убедись, что Армитедж получит то, за что платит.

Терзибашджан вздрогнул. Кейс увидел, что это серьезно повредило ему палец.

Над городом рассветал, когда Моли направила Мерседес к Топкапи. Финн и мощный турок по имени Махмуд подняли еще не совсем при сознании Ривьеру с дороги. Несколько минут спустя в аллюю въехал пыльный Цитрон, чтобы забрать армянина, который едва не потерял сознание.

Терзибашджан посмотрел на нее с вызовом. — Но нас все равно ждет, — ответила она. Она толкнула его внутрь и закрыла дверь. — Если ты еще раз появишься передо мной, я сделаю тебя холодным, — сказала она через стекло. Цитрон исчез по узким улицам.

Теперь Мерседес мчался по пробуждающемуся Стамбулу. Они проехали терминал туннеля Бейоглу и мчались по пешеходным дорожкам вдоль заброшенных апартаментных зданий, которые напоминали Кейсу Париж.

— Что это? — спросил он Моли, когда Мерседес остановился у парка, окружающего Серай. Он пытался осмотреть странное сочетание архитектурных стилей, которые формировали Топкапи.

— Это было частным борделем для султана, — объяснила она. — Там всегда было много женщин. Теперь это музей. Что-то вроде магазина финна, наполненного всем на свете: большими алмазами, мечами, левой рукой Иоанна Крестителя...

— В растворе?

— Нет. Мертвые. Их держали в металлической коробке с шарнирами, чтобы христиане могли поцеловать их для счастья. Христиане отняли это у них много веков назад и даже не очищают его, потому что это реликвия неверных.

Черные, ржавые олени медлили в парке Серая. Кейс шел рядом с ней, наблюдая, как кончики ее сапог прокалывают непроработанную траву, которая застыла от первого мороза. Они шли по тропинке из холодных, восьмиугольных камней. Зима ждала где-то на Балканах.

— Этот Терзибашджан — настоящий цыган, — сказала она. — Секретная полиция. Жестокий пытчик. Его легко купить, особенно с деньгами, которые Армитедж ему предложил.

Птицы начали щебетать в окружающих сухих деревьях.

— Я сделал эту работу для тебя, — сказал Кейс, — дело с Лондоном. Я что-то нашел, но не могу много с этим сделать.

Он рассказал ей историю о Корто.

— Ну, я знал, что "Скриминг Фист" не был связан с Армитеджем, — сказала она. — Я проверила.

Она погладила по ржавой спине бронзовой оленицы. — Думаешь, компьютер вытащил его оттуда? В французской психиатрической больнице?

— Зиммут, я так думаю, — ответил Кейс.

Кейс взял брошюру и положил её на стол. Это был дорогой буклет на французском, английском и турецком языках. — «Фрисайд — почему ждать?» Для четверки был забронирован рейс THY из аэропорта Йешилькой. Он был продуктом радиоактивного ядра старого Бонна, окружённого мусорными свалками. В магазин вошли три улыбающиеся японские туристки, которые вежливо кивнули Армитеджу. Армитедж быстро прошел через магазин, слишком явно, чтобы стоять рядом с Ривьерой. Ривьера повернулся и посмотрел на Кейса.

He smiled at him. He was very handsome; Case suspected behind his features the work of a Chiba surgeon. Subtle workmanship, not to be compared with Armitage's murky mix of beautiful faces. The man had a high, smooth forehead, his grey eyes were calm and distant. His nose, originally perhaps too evenly shaped, seemed broken and roughly remodelled. A touch of brutality was compensated by his delicate chin and his hasty smile. His teeth were small, even and very white. Case watched as his bright hands glided over the imitated sculpture fragments. Riviera did not behave like someone who had been attacked the night before, poisoned with an arrow, dragged away, subjected to Finn's tests and forced by Armitage to join his team. Case looked at his watch. Molly should be back soon from her drug run. He turned again to Riviera. "I bet you're already high as a kite, you arsehole," he said to the Hilton foyer. A grey-haired Italian matron in a white leather spencer jacket lowered her Porsche glasses and gawked. He grinned widely, stood up and hung his bag over his shoulder. He needed cigarettes for the flight. He wondered if there was a smoking compartment on the JAL shuttle. "Goodbye, Lady," he said to the aunt who quickly pushed her sunglasses back onto her nose and walked away. There were cigarettes in the souvenir shop, but he had no desire to talk to Armitage or Riviera. He left the foyer and discovered an automated console in a narrow niche at the end of a row of pay phones. 129

He rummaged through the handful of Lirasi in his trouser pocket and inserted one after another of the small coins from a dull alloy into the drop slot, where the anachronism of this process amused him somewhat. The next phone rang. He answered automatically. "Yes?" A faint harmonium, distant voices whispered on an orbital connection. Then a rush like a gust of wind: "Hello, Case." A 50-Lirasi coin fell from his hand and rolled across the Hilton carpet. "Wintermute, Case. It's time we talk. " It was a chip voice. "Do you want to say something, Case?" He hung up. On his way back to the foyer - he no longer thought about cigarettes - he had to pass by the row of phones. Each one rang in turn as he walked past - but each only once. 130

THIRD PART Midnight on Rue Jules Verne 131

Archipelago. The islands. Tonis, Spindle, Heap. Human DNA, like an oil film flowing from a deep gravitational well. Call up a graphic that roughly simplifies data exchange in the L-5 archipelago. A segment moves into view as a red body, a massive rectangle that fills the entire screen. Freeside. Freeside means many things, of which tourists passing through the shaft do not always catch all. Freeside is a brothel and business world, amusement park and free port, border town and health resort. Freeside is Las Vegas and the Hanging Gardens of Babylon, orbital Geneva and home to a carefully bred inbred family, the Tessier and Ashpool industrial clan. On the THY flight to Paris they sat together in first class. Molly was at the window seat, next to her Case, then Riviera and Armitage in the aisle. As the plane flew over water, Case saw a glittering Greek island city. And when he reached for his drink, he noticed in his bourbon with water a wriggling thing that reminded him of a giant human sperm. Molly leaned over him and gave Riviera an earful. "No tricks, baby. If you play these games with me, I'll give you a good smack on the ass. I can do it without breaking anything. Have fun." Case involuntarily glanced to the side to see Armitage's reaction. His face was calm, his blue eyes alert but without anger. "She's right, Peter. Leave it alone!" Case turned away again, only to catch a fleeting glimpse of a black rose whose petals shimmered like leather and whose stem gleamed with chrome. Peter Riviera put on a sweet smile, closed his eyes and fell asleep instantly. Molly turned away; the lenses reflected in the dark window. "You've been there before, haven't you?" she asked as he squeezed himself back into his white foam couch in the JAL shuttle.  "No. I don't travel much, just for business." The steward attached electrodes to his wrist and left ear.  "Hope you don't get SAS," she said.  "Air sickness? Not at all."  "Not the same thing. Your heartbeat speeds up in zero gravity, and your inner ear clicks for a while. It sets off your flight reflex as if you got a signal to run like hell, and it pumps out a lot of adrenaline." The steward moved on to Riviera and removed new electrodes from his red plastic apron. Case glanced over and tried to make out the contours of the old Orly terminal, but the shuttle ramp was shielded by very dynamic pressure defects in wet concrete.  The person at the window wore an Arabic slogan in red spray paint. He closed his eyes and told himself that the shuttle was just a big plane flying very high. It smelled like a plane, new clothes, chewing gum and exhaust fumes. He listened to the transmitted Koto music* and waited. Twenty minutes, and gravity hit him like a soft giant's hand with bones from ancient stone. Space adaptation syndrome** was worse than Molly had described it, but it passed fairly quickly so he could soon sleep. The steward woke him when they were ready to dock at the JAL terminal.  "Now transfer to Freeside?" he asked as he eyed a strand of Yeheyuan tobacco gracefully floating ten centimetres in front of his nose from his shirt pocket. Smoking was not allowed on shuttle flights.  "No, just another little quirk in our boss's plans, you know. We're taking a taxi to Zion, the Zion heap." By pressing a button she released her seatbelt and wriggled out of the foam cushion.  "Strange meeting place if you ask me," she said.  "Why?"  "Terrible. Rastas. The colony is already about thirty years old."  "What does that mean?"  "You'll see. I have nothing against the place. After all, you're allowed to smoke there." Zion had been founded by five workers who didn't want to return home, who turned their backs on the shaft and started building. They had suffered calcium loss and heart shrinkage before rotational gravity was established in the colony's midtorus. From the taxi dome, Zion looked like a makeshift settlement built around a central tower. * Koto = Japanese string instrument. - The translation ** Space adaptation syndrome = System complex due to stress, difficulties adapting to space; Space = Space. - The translation 134

Тональная искажение, как они это называли, — смесь из огромных архивов цифровой музыки из подводного мира, превращённая в чувственное мозаичное полотно. Это служило для поклонения, сказала Молли, и создавало чувство единства.

Он свободно держал его в руке. В другой он держал прозрачную мешочок с синей-зеленой водорослью. Он моргнул и улыбнулся. "Попробуй", — сказал Кейс. Улыбка застыла на его лице. Его глаза были отведены, а щеки покраснели. "Я сделал себе мембрану прямо над веной, так что мне не нужно беспокоиться о игле", — сказал он. "Это больно?" "Это ясно", — сказала Молли. "Эффект горности при уменьшении", — объяснил Армитедж. "Территория становится, казалось бы, более крутой, скалистой, но ее легко преодолевать. Чем выше ты поднимаешься, тем меньше гравитация". Он указал. "Велодром", — сказал он. "Высокопрофильная резина для низкой гравитации, достигающая скорости до ста километров в час". "Нас это сейчас не интересует", — сказал Армитедж, по-прежнему серьезно. "Жаль", — сказала Молли. "Я страстная велосипедистка". Ривьера рассмеялся. Армитедж пошел к другому концу проекции. "Это так. Детали голограммы здесь заканчиваются, а последняя часть спинделя пуста". "Это вилла Страйтлайт. Стремительный подъем из гравитационного поля, и любое приближение хитроумно. Армитедж ударил по ним, как по москитам. "Питер", — сказал Армитедж, — ты будешь первым, кто узнает. Найди приглашение. Как только ты войдешь, убедись, что Молли тоже входит!" Кейс смотрел в пустоту, воплощавшую Страйтлайт, и вспомнил историю финна: Смит, Джимми, говорящий головой, Ниндзя. "Можно узнать больше?" — спросил Ривьера. "Я должен спланировать свой наряд, знаешь". "Проверь улицы", — сказал Армитедж и вернулся к центру модели. "Улица Жюль Верна здесь. Там Rue Jules Verne", — пробормотал Ривьера. She pressed herself shivering to him as the stick flared and cast their united shadows on the bunker wall. Later, when he lay next to her, his hand between her thighs, he remembered how she had stood on the beach with foam playing around her ankles, and then he remembered what she'd said. "He told me you were coming," he whispered. But she only hugged herself to him, pressed her face against his thighs, put her hand on his, and murmured something in a dream. The music, at first sounding like his own heartbeat, woke him. He sat up next to her, pulled his jacket over his shoulders in the morning chill. Gray dawn light filtered through the entrance. The fire was long gone.  Ghostly hieroglyphs crawled across his field of vision, transparent stroke symbols forming before the neutral background of the bunker wall. He studied his palm, saw faint neon flickers moving beneath the skin according to an unknowable code. He raised his right hand and moved it cautiously. A faint, fading glow left a trail of flickering afterimages.  Hair on his arms and neck stood up. He sat there with bared teeth, listening for the music. His pulse skipped, then resumed... "What's wrong?" She sat up, wiped hair from her eyes. "Baby..." "I feel... like I'm tripping... Can you handle this?" She shook her head, reached for him, put her hands on his upper arms.  "Linda, who told you? Who said he'd come?"  "On the beach," she said, and something forced her to look away. "A boy. About thirteen. Lives there."  "What did he say?"  "He said you were coming. He said you wouldn't hate me. He said we'd be all right here, and explained where the rain hole was. He looks like a Mexican."  "Brazilian," Case said as another set of symbols streamed across the wall. "From Rio, I think." He stood up and pulled on his jeans.  "Case," she said in a shaky voice, "where are you going?"  "I'm going to find that boy," he said as the music started again: still just a steady, familiar beat that he couldn't quite place.  "Not, Case," she said.  "I think I saw something when I came here. A city below the beach. But it wasn't there yesterday. Have you seen it?" He zipped up his jacket and fumbled with his impossible shoelaces; finally, he kicked off his shoes and threw them in a corner. She nodded, looking down. "Yes," she said, "I see that sometimes."  "Have you been there, Linda?" He slipped into his jacket.  "No," she said, "but I've tried to go there."

Ich war noch nicht lange hier, da langweilte mich alles. Ich dachte mir, wenn es eine Stadt ist, könnte ich vielleicht etwas Interessantes finden. Sie verzog das Gesicht. Ich war gar nicht krank, ich wollte einfach nur etwas. Also packte ich Essen in eine Dose, rührte es flüssig um, weil ich keine zweite Dose für Wasser hatte. Und dann ging ich den ganzen Tag lang. Manchmal sah ich die Stadt. Sie schien nicht weit entfernt zu sein, aber sie kam nicht näher. Dann kam sie näher und ich erkannte, was das war. An diesem Tag sah es manchmal wie eine Ruine oder ein verlassener Ort aus, und manchmal glaubte ich, den Lichtkegel einer Maschine zu sehen, von Autos oder so...

Ihre Stimme versagte. "Und was ist es?"

"Das hier." Sie zeigte auf das Feuer, die dunklen Wände, den Morgengrauen, das durch den Eingang drang. "Unsere Behausung. Je näher man kommt, desto kleiner wird sie."

Er hielt noch einmal am Eingang an. "Hast du danach gefragt?"

"Ja. Er sagte, ich würde es nicht verstehen und meine Zeit verschwenden. Es sei irgendwie... ein Ereignis. Unser Horizont."

Mit diesen Begriffen konnte Case nichts anfangen. Er verließ den Bunker und ging ohne Ziel los, während er sich irgendwie vom Meer entfernte. Jetzt breiteten sich die Hieroglyphen auf dem Sand aus und wichen seinen Schritten aus. "He", sagte er, "es bricht zusammen. Ich wette, du weißt das auch. Was ist es, Kuang? Zerreißt dir ein chinesischer Eisbrecher das Herz? Ist die Dixie Flatline doch ein harter Brocken für dich, was?"

Er hörte ihren Namen rufen. Er schaute sich um und sah sie, wie sie ihm nachlief, ohne ihn einzuholen zu versuchen. Der kaputte Reißverschluss ihrer französischen Hose flatterte vor ihrem braunen Bauch, und Schamhaar blinzelte durch den Riss. Sie sah aus wie ein zum Leben erwachtes Mädchen aus alten Finn-Magazinen im Metro Holografix, nur war sie müde und traurig und ganz Mensch; in ihrem zerrissenen Gewand stolperte sie über das salzig-silbrige Seegras, machte ein klägliches Bild.

Und dann standen sie irgendwie im Wasser, alle drei, und das Zahnfleisch des Jungen leuchtete breit und rosa aus seinem braunen Gesicht. Er trug zerknitterte, farblose Shorts, und seine Glieder wirkten schlaksig vor dem fließenden Blaugrau der Wellen.

"Ich kenne dich", sagte Case an Lindas Seite. "Nein", antwortete der Junge mit einer piepsigen, melodischen Stimme, "du kennst mich nicht."

"Du bist die andere KI. Du bist Rio. Du willst Wintermute aufhalten. Wie heißt du? Dein Turing-Code?"

Der Junge machte einen Handstand in den Wellen und lachte. Er ging auf den Händen und sprang dann aus dem Wasser. Seine Augen waren wie Rivieras, aber nicht feindselig. "Um einen Dämon zu beschwören, muss man seinen Namen kennen. Einst träumten die Menschen davon, und jetzt ist es Wirklichkeit geworden. Das weißt du, Case. Es ist deine Aufgabe, die Namen von Programmen herauszufinden, die langen, formellen Namen, die ihre Besitzer zu verbergen suchen. Wahre Namen..."

"Ein Turing-Code ist kein Name."

"Neuromancer", sagte der Junge mit verkniffenen, grauen Augen zur aufgehenden Sonne. "Der Weg ins Reich der Toten. Dort bist du, mein Freund. Mylady Marie-France hat diesen Weg bereitet, aber Mylord hat sie erwürgt, bevor ich ihr Tagebuch einsehen konnte. Neuro von Nerven, die silbernen Pfade. Romancer. Romantiker. Nekromant. Ich rufe die Toten. Aber nein, mein Freund", und der Junge führte einen kleinen Tanz auf, stampfte mit seinen braunen Füßen im Sand, "ich bin die Toten und ihr Reich." Er lachte. Eine Möwe schrie.

"Bleib! Wenn dein Mädchen ein Gespenst ist, weiß es das nicht. Und du auch nicht."

"Du zerbrichst. Das Eis bricht."

"Nein", sagte er plötzlich traurig und ließ die schmächtigen Schultern hängen. Er scharrte mit dem Fuß im Sand. "Es ist viel einfacher. Aber du hast die Wahl." Die grauen Augen sahen Case ernst an. Eine neue Reihe von Symbolen huschte Zeile für Zeile durch sein Sichtfeld. Der Junge hinter ihnen flimmerte wie Luft über heißem Asphalt. Die Musik war jetzt laut, und Case konnte fast den Text hören: "Case, mein Schatz", sagte Linda und berührte ihn am Schultergelenk.

"Nein", sagte er. Er zog seine Jacke aus und reichte sie ihr. "Ich weiß es nicht", sagte er, "vielleicht bist du hier. Auf jeden Fall wird es kalt."

Er wandte sich ab und ging davon, und nach dem siebten Schritt schloss er die Augen und lauschte der Musik, die sich im Zentrum alles Seins artikulierte. Einmal schaute er zurück, öffnete aber nicht die Augen. Das war auch nicht nötig. Sie standen am Rand des Meeres. Linda Lee und der schmächtige Junge, der sagte, sein Name sei Neuromancer.

Seine Lederjacke hing in ihrer Hand, der Reißverschluss baumelte ins Wasser. Er ging weiter, folgte der Musik. Maelcums Zion-Sound. Da war ein grauer Ort mit feinen, fließenden Rastermustern, die abgestufte Halbtöne moirierten, erzeugt von einem einfachen Grafikprogramm. Da war ein langes Standbild eines Blickes durch Maschendraht mit Möwen, deren Flügel über dunklem Wasser erstarrt waren. Da waren Stimmen. Da war eine schwarze Spiegelfläche, die sich neigte, und er ein Tropfen Quecksilber, der herunterplätschte, sich in einem unsichtbaren Labyrinth verfing, zerplatzte, zusammenfloss, wieder zerplatzte...

"Case, bist du da?" Die Musik.

"Wieder da, bist du?" Die Musik drang aus seinen Ohren heraus.

"Wie lange?" hörte er sich fragen und spürte, wie trocken sein Mund war.

"Fünf Minuten vielleicht", dachte er, "zu lang. Ich wollte den Stecker ziehen, aber Stummer sagte nein. Das Bild wurde komisch, Stummer sagte, ich soll dir Kopfhörer aufsetzen."

Er öffnete die Augen. Über Maelcums Zügen lagen transparente Hieroglyphenzeilen. "Und deine Medizin", sagte Maelcum. "Zwei Derms."

Er lag auf dem Rücken am Boden der Bibliothek unter dem Monitor. Der Zionit half ihm, sich aufzusetzen, aber als er sich bewegte, schoss das Betaphenethylamin voll durch seinen Körper. Die beiden blauen Derms an seinem linken Handgelenk brannten. "Überdosierung", brachte er hervor.

"Komm schon, du!", rief man und starke Hände zogen ihn hoch. "Wir müssen gehen."

22. Механическая тележка завыла. Бетапинетиламин придал ей голос. Она больше не стояла на месте. В переполненной галерее, в длинных коридорах или за черным стеклянным порталом к Т-А-Крипте, своде, где холод медленно проник в сны Ашпула, поездка казалась бесконечной для Кейс, который не мог отличить движение тележки от безумного давления дозы. Когда, наконец, тележка вышла из строя и что-то белоснежное вспыхнуло под сиденьем, завывание прекратилось. На расстоянии трех метров от входа в пещеру Джейн остановилась тележка.

«Как далеко еще?» — Мэлком помог Кейсу выбраться из тележки, пока встроенный огнетушитель в камере двигателя транспортного средства не взорвался, и пепел из вентиляционных отверстий и отверстий для обслуживания вырвался наружу. Браун упал с задней части сиденья и хромая на искусственном песке удалился, тянув за собой бесполезную конечность. «Тебе нужно идти», — сказал Мэлком, беря конструкцию и крышу и надевая ремень безопасности на плечо. Ноги Кейса висели на шее Мэлкома, который следовал за ним. Их ждали голограммы Ривьеры, сцены пыток и каннибальские дети. Тройное изображение разрушило Молли. Ционит не обращал на них внимания.

«Медленно», — сказал Кейс, пытаясь идти в ногу. «Мы должны все сделать правильно». Мэлком остановился, повернулся к Кейсу и посмотрел ему в глаза, держа в руке Ремингтон. «Правильно, да?» — спросил он. — «Что правильное?»

Хидео вышел из тени, прицелив третий стрелок в узкую арку бамбука. Он склонился. Мэлком, сжимая стальной лук, широко развел глаза.

Кейс улыбнулся. Ривьера не знал о симстим-устройстве. В спешке получить доступ к принесенным наркотикам он это упустил. Но как Хидео мог этого не заметить? He had to think about Molly, lying on the black temper foam of the giant bed at the Hyatt. He went back to the bar and took out a chilled bottle of Danish vodka from the compartment. "Case," he said, holding the cold, slippery glass in one hand and the steel shuriken in the other, turning around. The Finn's face appeared on the massive Cray wall screen in the room. He could make out the pores on the man's nose. His yellow teeth were as big as pillows.

"I'm not Wintermute anymore."

He drank from the bottle and felt nothing. "I am the Matrix, Case."

Case laughed. "And what does that get you?"

"Nothing and everything. The sum of the system, the whole show, that's me."

"Did 3Jane's mother want that?"

"It's not different. It is what it is."

"But what do you do? Just sit there?" Case shrugged, put down the vodka and shuriken on the shelf, and lit a Yeheyuan. "I talk to my peers."

"But you are the whole thing. Talking to yourself?"

"There are others. I've already found one. A series of transfers recorded over eight years in the 1970s. There was no one before me who could make sense of it or answer questions about it."

"Where?"

"Centauri system."

"Oh," Case said, "Really? No bullshit?" And the screen went dark. He left the vodka on the shelf. He packed his things. She had bought him a lot of clothes he didn't really need, but something prevented him from just leaving them lying around. He was closing the last of his expensive calfskin travel bags when he remembered the shuriken. He moved the bottle out of the way and picked it up, her first gift.

"No," he said, throwing it. The star spun silver-bright from his hand and embedded itself in the wall screen. The monitor came on, displaying chaotic images flickering back and forth as if the thing were trying to shake something off that was causing it pain.

"I don't need you," he said. He spent most of his balance on a new pancreas and liver, the rest on a new Ono-Sendai and a one-way ticket home to the Sprawl. He found work. He found a girl named Michael. And one night in October, as he hacked through the scarlet layers of the Eastern Seaboard Fission Authority, he saw three tiny, incredible figures standing almost at the edge of one of the vast data staircases. Small as they were, he could make out the grin on the boy's face, his pink gums, the glint of the elongated gray eyes that had once belonged to Riviera. Linda was still wearing his jacket; she waved as he passed.

The third figure, standing close behind her and putting an arm around her shoulder, was him. Somewhere nearby laughter echoed, but it wasn't laughter. Molly, he would never see again.

Vancouver, July 1983

**Neuromancers: A Postscript by Norman Spinrad**

It seems we have, for the first time in decades, a new literary movement within science fiction and a categorization of SF authors that predates the "New Wave" of the 1960s. I mean, of course, the so-called "Cyberpunks," though I prefer to call these writers "Neuromancers," as Tappan King suggested, for several reasons. While some of my motives for this renaming may seem trivial, others touch upon the core of the matter, and while a rose by any other name smells just as sweet, it is difficult to imagine the Third Reich raising its fists and saluting in unison: "Heil Schickelgruber!"

There is agreement that the catalyst for this movement was William Gibson's novel *Neuromancer*, and the term "Cyberpunk" comes from Gardner Dozois, who does not consider himself a Cyberpunk or seek to be labeled as such. The term "New Wave," on the other hand, originated with critic Judith Merril, who was passionately involved in 1960s SF but played no essential part in its development. If we need a label, then let it be "Neuromancers," derived from the title of a central work of this movement; a term untainted by prejudice.

336
---CHUNK-SEPARATOR---
Hafen und der exaktere beschreibt, worum es sich bei diesem Phänomen handelt, wie wir gleich sehen wer den. Aber brauchen wir überhaupt einen Namen? Schliesslich konnte man sich in den sechziger Jahren paradoxerweise gerade dadurch als eingeschriebenes Mitglied der „New Wave“ identifizieren, indem man die Existenz des Phänomens New Wave leugnete. Die New Wave war laut den meisten von uns den New Wave zugehörigen Autoren eine nachträgliche Benennung seitens der Kritiker für Autoren, die stilistisch, ideologisch oder auch philosophisch nicht viel mehr gemeinsam hatten als ihr Eintreten für Vielfalt. Eine solche Sammlung bilderstürmerischer Individualisten als New Wave-Bewegung zu bezeichnen war gewissermaßen dasselbe wie von geordneter Anarchie zu sprechen. Zwischen der New Wave der sechziger und der Neuromantischen Bewegung der achtziger Jahre gibt es durchaus viele Ähnlichkeiten, allerdings fällt die Ablehnung einer gemeinsamen Weltanschauung nicht darunter.

Es gibt natürlich einen harten Kern von Autoren der achtziger Jahre, die für eine gemeinsame literarische Verwandtschaft eintreten und sich manchmal sogar „Cyberpunks“ nennen lassen. Unter diesem Etikett präsentierten einige davon beim ziemlich chaotischen Forum der North American Science Fiction Convention 1985 in Austin, Texas ihre Werke. Darunter waren Bruce Sterling, John Shirley, Lewis Shiner, Pat Cadigan und Rudy Rucker. William Gibson war sicherlich im Geiste anwesend, wenn auch nicht persönlich, wie es sich für einen Neuromantiker gebührt. Andere Autoren, die als Wegbereiter oder Weggefährten gelten, sind unter anderem Alfred Bester, Fritz Leiber, Cordwainer Smith, K.W. Jeter und der Verfasser dieser Zeilen. Während es kein Fiktion-Magazin gibt, das den Neuromantikern als Forum dient, wie New Worlds damals für die New Wave, und keinen dominanten Herausgeber wie Michael Moorcock, haben wir ein kritisches Neuromantisches Periodikum namens Cheap Truth, das unter Pseudonym in Austin erscheint, und Ellen Datlow, Fiktion-Redakteurin bei Omni, die sich gelegentlich als „Queen of Punk SF“ bezeichnet hat und viele Geschichten dieser Autoren, darunter Gemeinschaftsproduktionen, veröffentlicht hat.

Wir haben es also tatsächlich mit einer echten literarischen Strömung zu tun, deren Existenz zudem von vielen ihrer maßgeblichen Vertreter bekräftigt wird. Allerdings steckt hinter der Bezeichnung „Cyberpunk“ mehr als nur die Arbeit der Autoren, die sich bisher als „Cyberpunks“ bezeichnet haben. Der logische Einstieg für eine Diskussion der Neuromantischen Bewegung liegt natürlich bei William Gibsons Roman Neuromancer, der mit dem Hugo, Nebula, Locus und Philip K. Dick Award ausgezeichnet wurde und als Maßstab für den harten Kern von Autoren gilt. Es ist das Buch, auf das die Bezeichnung „Cyberpunk“ am ehesten zutrifft.

Case, der Held von Neuromancer, hat zweifellos eine Punk-Einstellung im heutigen Sinne. Er ist ein Ex-Speed-Freak, wobei das „Ex“ nicht freiwillig, sondern im Rahmen einer „therapeutischen“ Maßnahme gegen seinen Willen zustandegekommen ist. Er steht am Rande der Gesellschaft und agiert hart an der Grenze zur Unterwelt seiner Zukunft, und seine gelegentliche Geliebte ist eine gnadenlose Killerin mit permanent implantierter Spiegeloptik.

Bis jetzt könnten wir es mit einem ganz typischen Protagonisten von Harlan Ellison zu tun haben. Und tatsächlich ist ein starker Ellison-Einfluss in der Neuromantischen Ästhetik spürbar. Denn es war Ellison, der in seine Science-Fiction das heutige Bannden- oder Straßenmilieu einbrachte und damit mehr als jeder andere dafür sorgte, dass die Stimmung, der Stil, der Rhythmus und die Charaktere der Halbwelt der Straße in die saubere, mittelständische weiße Welt der Science-Fiction der fünfziger Jahre eindrangen, obwohl der Anschein besteht, dass Gibson ebenso unmittelbar von William Burroughs beeinflusst wurde.

Sicherlich wimmelt es im Werk Ellisons von Punk-Protagonisten in der Bedeutung der fünfziger Jahre. Aber gegen Ende der siebziger Jahre hatte „Punk“ eine neue Bedeutung gewonnen, obwohl seltsamerweise schwarze Lederjacken und mutige Frisuren zu Attributen des Aufbegehrens wurden. Die Punks der fünfziger Jahre rebellierten gegen Mom, Apple Pie, sexuelle Unterdrückung, Intellektualität und das Amerika von Dwight Eisenhower und Norman Rockwell, und ihre erotisierende Marschmusik – die frühen Anfänge von Rock and Roll auf der Bühne – war interessanterweise bereits primitiv elektronisch.

Denen Punks der fünfziger Jahre verschwand in den sechziger Jahren die Bühne. Ihr Schicksal war besiegelt, als Bob Dylan und die Beatles mit der Umwandlung des Rock and Roll von der fetzigen Elvis- und Straßengang-Musik in einen politisch bewussten, transzendentalen, revolutionären Utopismus begannen, der die Gegenkultur einläutete.

Die „Punks“ oder „New Waver“, die Mitte der siebziger Jahre auftauchten, waren trotz ihrer äußeren Erscheinung keineswegs dasselbe wie die Punks der fünfziger Jahre, denn die neuen Punks rebellierten gegen das Gegenkultur-Gefühl der sechziger Jahre, nicht gegen die längst verschwundene, unschuldige Langeweile der fünfziger.

Wogegen sie rebellierten? Gegen den eitlen Kunstanspruch der Rockmusik der frühen 70er, den gescheiterten, lässigen Utopismus der Gegenkultur, den Mystizismus und den naiven Glauben, die Zukunft würde besser, wenn der jugendliche Idealismus am Ruder bliebe. Wenn die Punks der fünfziger tatsächlich anti-intellektuelle Rebellen waren, dann waren die neuen Punks der siebziger intellektuelle Anti-Intellektuelle; keine naiven, natürlich nihilistischen Rebellen ohne Ziel und Richtung, sondern selbstbewusste nihilistische Pessimisten, die den Zynismus zu einer mehr oder weniger kompakten Philosophie erhoben und wussten, was sie taten.

Case von Neuromancer ist ein „Punk“ im neuen Sinne, ein intellektueller Punk, kein einfacher Greaser. Die „Cyber“-Hälfte der Gleichung bestimmt seine Intelligenz. Genau dadurch ist Neuromancer eine Wasserscheide in der Science-Fiction zwischen New Wave und Neuromantik und erhellt, was die Neuromantische Strömung für die Science-Fiction bedeutet.

Case ist der „Neuromancer“ des Titels, und der Name ist natürlich ein Wortspiel zu „Necromancer“ (dt. Nekromant), was Geisterbeschwörer bedeutet, und „neu*ro“, also zum Nervensystem gehörend. Der Neuromancer ist ein zeitgenössischer (oder in diesem Fall in der nahen Zukunft angesiedelter) Zauberer, dessen Hexerei darin besteht, sein protoplasmisches Nervensystem mit dem elektronischen Nervensystem der Computerwelt zu interfizieren und imagistisch zu manipulieren (und selbst * Interface: unmittelbare wechselseitige Verbindung, direkter Kontakt – Der Übers. 341
---CHUNK-SEPARATOR---
Being manipulated (like traditional shamans who enter into a mystical interaction through drugs or trance) is not new in science fiction, as similar concepts can be found in my "Riding the Torch," Vernor Vinge's "True Namer," Alfred Bester's "Golem 100," and countless recent stories and novels where human protagonists act within a kind of "cyberspace" or cybernetic space. The Disney Studios even made a film brimming with special effects based on this idea: Tron. What is new, however, and aesthetically significant, is that Gibson's Neuromancer is not a computer freak or hacker, but a cyberpunk—a term he himself coined. While some hard science fiction authors criticize Gibson for his computer depictions falling short of scientific rigor, they inadvertently confirm the novel's status as a work of hard science fiction by subjecting it to such criticism. What truly bothers them, apart from scientific discrepancies, is that Neuromancer stylistically and characterologically diverges from previous hard science fiction novels. This seems to also irk the more literary types for their own reasons. Gibson writes hard science fiction, but not like Heinlein, Poul Anderson, Hal Clement, or Gregory Benford. Rather, he aligns with Ellison, William Burroughs, Michael Moorcock of "Jerry Cornelius" fame, and the spirit of "The Big Flash," "Bug Jack Barron," or "Street Meat." Neuromancer fuses New Wave and hard science fiction, despite appearing contradictory. It's neuromantic—neuro-romantic—yet also new romantic.

When Bob Dylan performed at the Newport Folk Festival in the early 1960s with an electric guitar, the Beats, Bohemians, and folkies were outraged. This was a folk festival; the attendees were intellectuals, Dylan was their hero, and his lyrics expressed what would later become the spirit of counterculture through instrumentation. However, the electric guitar was the instrument of rock 'n' roll—the music of bald-headed guys in black leather jackets, the rockers, skinheads, not mods who emerged from counterculture. It's hard to believe today that rock once contrasted with the intellectual, cultural, aesthetic, and societal values of the elite bohemian circle; that it was supposed to transform into the shared values of an entire generation through Dylan and the Beatles' influence—into counterculture! And this strange contradiction persisted in the heart of counterculture throughout the 1960s and well into the 1970s; their anti-technological ideology, neo-Luddism, distrust towards science and technology, bucolic mysticism, and ecological consciousness were all expressed through rock 'n' roll—a music style characterized by electric guitars and entirely electronic synthesizers.

The New Wave in science fiction was also a phenomenon of counterculture, and the intense conflict between New Wavers and the old guard reflected, on a literary level, the polarization and generation gap within society at large. We all know the story: The New Wave advocated for sexual liberation and cultural pluralism, while the old guard stood for traditional moral values. New Wave embraced stylistic experiments, whereas the old guard favored simple, transparent storytelling. The New Wave was anti-establishment and anti-war, while the old guard supported our troops in Vietnam. The old guard constantly accused the New Wave of being nihilistic towards science and technology, mocking their naive belief that advancements in science and technology would inherently improve human conditions. Meanwhile, the political polarization of the time led to a false dichotomy between science and spirit, between "hard" science fiction and stylistic experimentation, between logical positivism and "healthy" popular sentiment, between strictly scientifically-oriented and characterologically-based science fiction, between computer hackers and hippies, between the scientific worldview and romantic impulses.

It took a while for us to realize that this dichotomy is illusory. We should have seen or heard it from the start, because the expression of romantic impulses through "high tech" instruments is at the heart of rock 'n' roll. Rock has always been the music of libidinous anarchism and romantic and transcendental impulses; without this message, it's not rock 'n' roll. Of course, rock has always been defined as a technological music genre, as electric guitars and synthesizers are integral to its identity. The ideological politics of the 1960s and early 1970s obscured this obvious truth, clouding our judgment and hiding "high tech" romance and cyborg transcendentalism. On one hand, long natural hair, ecology, naturally-produced food, bucolic nature-romanticism, Eastern mysticism, and personal fulfillment. On the other, the hard, morally justified, militaristic, rational realm of science and technology, and a technocratic America.

However, in the mid-1970s, new punks rebelled against the anti-artificial, anti-technological aesthetics of counterculture. Earthy colors were out; black glossy leather and high-gloss chrome "high tech" was in. Pink nickel-framed glasses were replaced with tinted, reflective lenses. Long natural hair was out; bold, spiky, brightly-colored, over-the-top hairstyles were in. A new "high tech" romance emerged. The new punks are not technocratic slaves but anarchist rockers in the old romantic tradition. But they are rockers who embrace technology, just as their predecessors embraced freedom.
