Text to translate:
In this science and technology-dominated world, one readily accepts the reality of the last quarter of the twentieth century with its future prospects. So do the Neuromantics. People like Gibson and especially John Shirley are Cyberpunks in that their work is shaped by the trappings of contemporary rock style. Others such as Bruce Sterling, Lewis Shiner, and Greg Bear (who was quite surprised to find himself on a Cyberpunk forum in Austin) write stories set in an environment devoid of any punk aesthetics. Gibson, and even more so Sterling and Bear, conduct scientific extrapolations just as thoroughly as established "hard science fiction" authors like Larry Niven or Hal Clement and others. However, somehow none of the Neuromantics appear to consider scientific speculation as the be-all and end-all of their work and its core topic. While only a few strive for the characteristic sharpness of Gibson's prose, they all focus directly on character with an intensity and subtlety that clashes with the essence of traditional "hard science fiction". Nonetheless, the Neuromantics write "hard science fiction" according to any positive definition of the term and at the same time character studies. Indeed, what unites them all is their thematic approach, both in "hard science fiction" and in character-oriented SF, and thus the fundamental theme of every truly challenging SF: our increasingly intimate feedback relationship with the technosphere we create has changed, and will continue to change, what it means to be human.

John Shirley's latest novel, Eclipse, is more politically explicit than any other SF novel in the past fifteen years. Set at the beginning of the next century, it employs multiple perspectives to narrate the complex story of a complex resistance against a complex fascist conspiracy aiming to control the United States and Western Europe. In recent years, Shirley lived in Europe for a while and wrote political thrillers under various names, which is evident in Eclipse. One may not accept Shirley's politics without contradiction, but no SF author has confronted international political terrorism as realistically as Shirley; after all, future politics cannot be portrayed more realistically. Although Shirley does not name anyone, the currents and conspiracies in Eclipse are unmistakable derivations from existing trends.

Eclipse is what is so rare today: a furious, politically engaged SF novel, something Shirley makes no secret of. What makes Shirley a true Neuromantic? Well, for one thing, most of the characters in Eclipse, at least the sympathetic ones, come from the same borderline area under the belt line of "high tech" society as in Neuromancer, and Shirley's stylistic sensibility is generally more consciously streetwise than Gibson's. However, here the "punk feeling" is applied to a very profound and significant political analysis, so that if we accept politology as a science, we must recognize Eclipse as "hard science fiction" at its extreme.

While Eclipse is packed with conventional aggression and action, the decisive battle takes place in the Grid, as Shirley calls the international media network covering the Earth. The fascists use it cunningly. Society, however, cannot accept it. And resistance tries desperately to gain access. What is perceived through enhanced electronic senses has more psychological reality than actual events in the so-called real world and thus shapes political reality more reliably than physical force.

At the climax of Eclipse (yes, it's the first book in a trilogy), the fascist moloch relentlessly crushes the Arc de Triomphe, the very symbol of resistance. But Rickenharp, a rock musician and resistance fighter, has filled the arch with instruments, microphones, and amplifiers, and his final performance to the death before the world via the Grid transforms the fascist victory into a symbolic glorification of resistance, which Shirley makes us convincingly believe.

This is about as romantic as the Neuromantics, or rather Neo-Romantics, can get, and it's only fitting that the technical instruments of this victory of symbolism over raw, physical reality are microphones, amplifiers, and electric guitars of rock 'n' roll. Eclipse may be more of a Cyberpunk than a genuine Neuromantic novel because the "hard science" material is only peripherally addressed, but the absolutely central theme from rock 'n' roll to the climax is characteristic of an original Neuromantic sensibility. For Rickenharp's triumph at the end is a cyborg triumph made possible only through electronic amplification of his fleshly musicality, clearly demonstrating that cyborgs, romantic cyborgs, Neo-Romantic cyborgs, use technological enhancement for transcendental purposes since the day Dylan picked up the electric guitar.

As for typical music of our time, we have all accepted Neuromancy as given for a quarter century. And Eclipse goes even further. Rickenharp consumes a drug that enhances his musical creativity, albeit at physical cost. A red thread runs through the entire book: the connection between electronic amplification and transformation in chemically-stimulated humans, showing us what we already know: chemical consciousness alteration is also part of technology, it has long penetrated our whole society, and it's no coincidence that drugs and rock are so closely intertwined. Electronic amplification and consciousness-altering drugs have already changed the parameters of human sensing and thus the definition of human perception and psyche.

Other Neuromantics take technological change in our definitions of humanity even further. It is indeed the acceptance of technological evolution and change in our conception of humanity, not traditional mistrust against it, that ultimately determines the Neuromantic sensibility. Greg Bear, who pondered what a sophisticated "hard science" type like himself would have lost among Cyberpunks on that forum in Austin, unwittingly answered his question himself when he left all present stunned with the most radical Neo-Romantic statement of the entire event.
---CHUNK-SEPARATOR---
"How many of you believe that within fifty years people will be clearly recognizable as humans?" he asked the audience. A forest of raised hands. "You're all wrong," Bear explained with his usual calmness. In the short story version of Bear's "Blood Music", an experiment with biochips, meaning with DNA as a read-write computer memory, fails. The altered molecules become conscious and the consciousness rises to a pre-cellular level. These 'noocytes' spread like a disease and infect the population. Humans develop (or rather degenerate) into colony-forming organisms, each molecule endowed with human intelligence. Noocytes dissolve humanity ultimately into its composite molecules, thus causing humanity's disappearance, replaced by a new intelligent life form. A sort of scientific horror story. When Bear expanded the short story "Blood Music" into a novel, he became philosophical and a neo-romanticist. While the short story ends with the noocytes replacing humanity, the novel continues exploring the new noocyte world. The personalities of the vanished humans are duplicated, multiplied and stored at the noocyte level, preserved by noocytes as obscure heritage and thus making them many times immortal. We witness how a girl's intelligence is enhanced through the substitution of her personality into the 'noosphere'. The noosphere itself becomes a sort of transcendent software reality, perhaps a realm of illusion but also a higher state of existence, ultimately detached from the physical universe via the instruments of science and technology. Of course, even "Blood Music" is not the contemporary work that most perfectly expresses the neo-romantic ethos. While Bear lets human evolution converge into a transcendental endpoint where humanity is replaced by a higher consciousness that finally transcends the physical universe, this idea has many predecessors, starting from Olaf Stapledon and Arthur C. Clarke's "Childhood's End" to Hindu and Buddhist texts. Bruce Sterling's novel "Schismatrix", however, takes different paths and in its own way disrupts our familiar definitions of humanity even more radically. "Schismatrix" is the somewhat picaresque story of diplomat and rogue Abelard Lindsay, who wanders through space and time in the solar system. From a space colony in lunar orbit to the asteroid belt and outer satellites, Lindsay traverses a series of completely artificial worlds and at the same time offers readers deep insights into a good part of history. The historical dynamics of Sterling's solar system is determined by the long, sometimes hostile, sometimes mutually penetrating dialectic between 'Mechs' and 'Shapers'. The 'Mechanics' operate cyborg technology while the 'Formers' deal with genetic manipulation and biological transformation. Their endless conflicts are occasionally fought militarily, most often however economically, diplomatically, technologically and aesthetically, and as success and failure alternate on both sides, streams of deviants and refugees like Lindsay shift back and forth between the fronts. What eventually crystallizes out of all this is the title's schism, a solar system of bewildering human complexity where 'posthuman' and 'cladogenetic' play key roles. The posthuman is nothing more than the description of the situation that results after decades of genetic manipulation, cyborg practice, cloning and the combination of both species-altering technologies. The original human form has been so durably reshaped by these technologies that it essentially only exists in a colony on the moon orbit, set up as some sort of nature reserve. Posthumans do not regard this with horror but purely positively. The cladogenetic development is the most extreme current statement of the neo-romantic concept of human evolution through science and technology. In Bear's work, humanity first becomes a single posthuman physical form that then transcends the physical universe. Sterling, on the other hand, introduces the concept of evolutionary diversity through technology. Evolution à la Sterling proceeds cladogenetically or via daughter species, not linearly; it spreads out radially. Successful species do not evolve in a straight line into a single daughter genus but spread out radially into a variety of follow-up species. The fully developed schism harbors a complex variety of posthuman species, all the result of natural selection as well as technical development. There are 'Lobsters' that have been so integrated through cyborg procedures that they can't tolerate any atmosphere. There are humans biologically adapted to methane seas. An entire space colony has as its interior decoration altered protoplasm from a single woman, Lindsay's gentle lover, who retains her human personality. "Schismatrix" is a thoroughly worked-out 'hard science fiction' novel where the scientific and technical extrapolations and descriptions of space dwellers, without sparing any detail, are executed with care and meticulousness that would make Heinleins, Nivens or even Benfords proud. But while the prose follows the direct, transparent line we expect from the 'hard science' genre, Sterling uses it to equip his novel with equal psychological depth and detail. The characters, especially Lindsay, are convincingly human-psychologically depicted despite their bizarre physiognomies. And precisely that makes "Schismatrix" perhaps even more radical and stirring than "Blood Music". While Bear's single 'daughter species', the noosphere, is a mystical, transcendental endpoint that has its predecessors in Stapledon, Clarke and perhaps most directly in Teilhard de Chardin and primarily confronts us with our spiritual conception of humanity, Sterling's non-transcendent portrayal of...

Только выходя из относительно нормального и неизменного человеческого психического состояния, преобразованной физически, кладогенетической человечности, с неизбежным изменением нашего понятия о теле благодаря науке и технике, мы встретим инопланетян, и это будем мы сами. Только в самом конце Стерлинг предлагает некий трансцендентный выход, который сталкивается с четко очерченной структурой, которую он так тщательно построил, сделав Линдси эволюционным конечным пунктом, который не совсем убеждает нас, читателей, и, возможно, даже самого Стерлинга. В целом роман направлен на то, чтобы эволюция нашего вида благодаря науке и технике не имела конца, а была бесконечным процессом, раскрывающимся в новых ветвях. Таким образом, нейромантики сами, по крайней мере на этом этапе, опасаются последних последствий исследования границ технологически управляемой человеческой эволюции. Даже видящие нашей расы в настоящее время неспособны пересечь этот порог. Эволюция проходит кладогенетически так же, как и научная фантастика. Возможно, всегда будет порог, преодоление которого неизбежно зависит от подвида.
